> Nawaz: ... What does [killing the Yazidi population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinjar_massacre) on Mount Sinjar have to do with US foreign policy? What does enforcing [headscarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban_treatment_of_women) (tents in fact) on women in [Waziristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waziristan) and Afghanistan, and lashing them, forcing men to grow beards under threat of a whip, chopping off hands, and so forth, have to do with US foreign policy? \
> &#x20;Harris: This catalogue of irrelevancy could be extended indefinitely. What does the [Sunni bombing of Shia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Shi'ism#Pakistan) and [Ahmadi mosques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Ahmadis) in Pakistan have to with Israel or US foreign policy?[^cite_note-tolerance-2015-57-233]

Nawaz also argues that suicide bombers in non-Muslim majority countries such as the [7 July 2005 bombers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_July_2005_London_bombings) can be said to motivated by ideology not by any desire to compel UK military to withdraw from "their homeland", as they were born and raised in Yorkshire. They had never set foot in Iraq and do not speak its language.[^cite_note-Nawaz-debate-60]

### Socio-psychological motivations

#### Socio-psychological development

A motivator of violent radicalism (not just found in Al-Qaeda and ISIS) is psychological development during adolescence.[^cite_note-Rizvi-2016-88-234] Cally O'Brien found many terrorists were "not exposed to the West in a positive context, whether by simple isolation or conservative family influence, until well after they had established a personal and social identity." Looking at theories of psychological [personal identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_\(social_science\)) Seth Schwartz, Curitis Dunkel and Alan Waterman found two types of "personal identities" susceptible to radicalization leading to terrorism:

1. "Foreclosed and authoritarian" — Principally conservative Muslims who are often taught by their family and communities from early childhood to not deviate from a strict path and to either consider inferior or hate outside groups. When exposed to (alien) western culture, they are likely to judge it relative to their perception of the correct order of society, as well as perceive their own identities and mental health to be at risk.

   [^cite_note-235]

   [^cite_note-Identity_Theory_2009-236]

   [^cite_note-Rizvi-2016-88-234]

2. "Diffuse and aimless" — Principally converts whose lives are characterized by "aimlessness, uncertainty and indecisiveness" and who have neither explored different identities nor committed to a personal identity. Such people are "willing to go to their deaths for ideas \[such as jihadism] that they have appropriated from others" and that give their lives purpose and certainty.

   [^cite_note-Identity_Theory_2009-236]

   [^cite_note-Rizvi-2016-88-234]

### Characteristics of terrorists

In 2004, a forensic psychiatrist and former foreign service officer, [Marc Sageman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Sageman), made an "intensive study of biographical data on 172 participants in the jihad" in his book *Understanding Terror Networks*.[^cite_note-237] He concluded [social networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_network), the "tight bonds of family and friendship", rather than [emotional and behavioral disorders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotional_and_behavioral_disorders) of "poverty, trauma, madness, \[or] ignorance", inspired [alienated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_alienation) young Muslims to join the jihad and kill.[^cite_note-238]

According to anthropologist [Scott Atran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Atran), a [NATO](21133.md) researcher studying suicide terrorism, as of 2005, the available evidence contradicts a number of simplistic explanations for the motivations of terrorists, including mental instability, poverty, and feelings of humiliation.[^cite_note-burke-239] The greatest predictors of [suicide bombings](18835454.md#Post-Cold_War_and_war_on_terror)—one common type of terror tactic used by Islamic terrorists—turns out to be not religion but group dynamics. While personal humiliation does not turn out to be a motivation for those attempting to kill civilians, the perception that others with whom one feels a common bond are being humiliated can be a powerful driver for action. "Small-group dynamics involving friends and family that form the [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora) cell of [brotherhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraternity) and [camaraderie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camaraderie) on which the rising tide of martyrdom actions is based".[^cite_note-240] Terrorists, according to Atran, are social beings influenced by social connections and values. Rather than dying "for a cause", they might be said to have died "for each other".[^cite_note-burke-239]

In a 2011 doctoral thesis, anthropologist Kyle R. Gibson reviewed three studies documenting 1,208 suicide attacks from 1981 to 2007 and found that countries with higher [polygyny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygyny) rates correlated with greater production of [suicide terrorists](18835454.md#terrorism).[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242] Political scientist [Robert Pape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Pape) has found that among Islamic suicide terrorists, 97 percent were unmarried and 84 percent were male (or if excluding the [Kurdistan Workers' Party](69680.md), 91 percent male),[^cite_note-243] while a study conducted by the [U.S. military](32212.md) in [Iraq](7515928.md) in 2008 found that suicide bombers were almost always single men without children aged 18 to 30 (with a mean age of 22), and were typically students or employed in [blue-collar occupations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue-collar_worker).[^cite_note-244] In addition to noting that countries where polygyny is widely practiced tend to have higher [homicide rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_intentional_homicide_rate) and [rates of rape](5672810.md), political scientists [Valerie M. Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerie_M._Hudson) and Bradley Thayer have argued that because [Islam is the only major religious tradition where polygyny is still largely condoned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygyny_in_Islam), the higher degrees of marital inequality in [Islamic countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world) than most of the world causes them to have larger populations susceptible to suicide terrorism, and that [promises of harems of virgins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houri) for [martyrdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahid) serves as a mechanism to mitigate [in-group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In-group_and_out-group) conflict within Islamic countries between alpha and non-alpha males by bringing esteem to the latter's families and redirecting their violence towards out-groups.[^cite_note-Hudson_&_Thayer_2010-245]

Along with his research on the [Tamil Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_Tigers_of_Tamil_Eelam), Scott Atran found that [Palestinian](241405.md) terrorist groups (such as [Hamas](13913.md)) provide monthly [stipends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stipend), [lump-sum payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lump_sum), and massive prestige to the families of suicide terrorists.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] Citing Atran and other anthropological research showing that 99 percent of Palestinian suicide terrorists are male, that 86 percent are unmarried, and that 81 percent have at least six siblings (larger than the average Palestinian family size), cognitive scientist [Steven Pinker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Pinker) argues in *[The Better Angels of Our Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Better_Angels_of_Our_Nature)* (2011) that because the families of men in the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza](12047.md) often cannot afford [bride prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_price) and that many potential brides end up in polygynous marriages, the financial compensation of an act of suicide terrorism can buy enough brides for a man's brothers to have children to make the [self-sacrifice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altruism_\(biology\)) pay off in terms of [kin selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kin_selection) and [biological fitness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitness_\(biology\)) (with Pinker also citing a famous quotation attributed to evolutionary biologist [J. B. S. Haldane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._B._S._Haldane) when Haldane quipped that he would not sacrifice his life for his brother but would for "two brothers or eight cousins").[^cite_note-248]

In 2007, scholar [Olivier Roy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_Roy_\(professor\)) described the background of the hundreds of *global* (as opposed to local) terrorists who were incarcerated or killed and for whom authorities have records, as being surprising in a number of ways: The subjects frequently had a Westernized background; there were few [Palestinians](23267.md), [Iraqis](4184636.md), or [Afghans](737.md) "coming to avenge what is going on in their country"; there was a lack of religiosity before radicalization through being "born again" in a foreign country; a high percentage of subjects had converted to Islam; their backgrounds were "de-territorialized "—meaning, for example, they were "born in a country, then educated in another country, then go to fight in a third country and take refuge in a fourth country"; and their beliefs about jihad differed from traditional ones—i.e. they believed jihad to be permanent, global, and "not linked with a specific territory."[^cite_note-249] Roy believes terrorism/radicalism is "expressed in religious terms" among the terrorists studied because

1. most of the radicals have a Muslim background, which makes them open to a process of re-Islamisation ("almost none of them having been pious before entering the process of radicalisation"), and

   [^cite_note-roy-inside-71]

2. jihad is "the only cause on the global market". If you kill in silence, it will be reported by the local newspaper; "if you kill yelling 'Allahu Akbar', you are sure to make the national headlines". Other extreme causes—ultra-left or radical ecology are "too bourgeois and intellectual" for the radicals.

   [^cite_note-roy-inside-71]

Author [Lawrence Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Wright) described the characteristic of "[displacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Displacement_\(psychology\))" of members of the most famous Islamic terrorist group, al-Qaeda:

> What the recruits tended to have in common—besides their urbanity, their [cosmopolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmopolitanism) backgrounds, their education, their facility with languages, and their computer skills—was displacement. Most who joined the jihad did so in a country other than the one in which they were reared. They were Algerians living in [expatriate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expatriate) enclaves in France, [Moroccans](5639315.md) in Spain, or [Yemenis](350939.md) in Saudi Arabia. Despite their accomplishments, they had little standing in the host societies where they lived.[^cite_note-250]

This profile of global Jihadists differs from that found among more recent local Islamist suicide bombers in Afghanistan. According to a 2007 study of 110 suicide bombers by Afghan pathologist Dr. Yusef Yadgari, 80% of the attackers studied had some kind of physical or mental disability. The bombers were also "not celebrated like their counterparts in other Muslim nations. Afghan bombers are not featured on posters or in videos as martyrs."[^cite_note-251] Daniel Byman, a Middle East expert at the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution), and Christine Fair, an assistant professor in peace and security studies at [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University), argue that many of the Islamic terrorists are foolish and untrained, perhaps even untrainable, with one in two [Taliban](30635.md) suicide bombers killing only themselves.[^cite_note-Byman-252]

Studying 300 cases of people charged with jihadist terrorism in the United States since 11 September 2001, author [Peter Bergen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Bergen) found the perpetrators were "generally motivated by a mix of factors", including "militant Islamist ideology;" opposition to "American foreign policy in the Muslim world; a need to attach themselves to an ideology or organization that gave them a sense of purpose"; and a "[cognitive opening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_opening)" to militant Islam that often was "precipitated by personal disappointment, like the death of a parent".[^cite_note-Bergen-15-6-2016-253]

However, two studies of the background of Muslim terrorists in Europe—one of the UK and one of France—found little connection between religious piety and terrorism among the terrorist rank and file. A "restricted" report of hundreds of case studies by the UK domestic counter-intelligence agency [MI5](100076.md) found that

> \[f]ar from being religious zealots, a large number of those involved in terrorism do not practise their faith regularly. Many lack religious literacy and could actually be regarded as religious novices. Very few have been brought up in strongly religious households, and there is a higher than average proportion of converts. Some are involved in drug-taking, drinking alcohol and visiting prostitutes. MI5 says there is evidence that a well-established religious identity actually protects against violent radicalisation.[^cite_note-Travis-20-8-2008-68]

A 2015 "general portrait" of "the conditions and circumstances" under which people living in France become "Islamic radicals" (terrorists or would-be terrorists) by Olivier Roy (see above) found radicalisation was not an "uprising of a Muslim community that is victim to poverty and racism: only young people join, including converts".[^cite_note-roy-inside-71]

## Refutations, criticisms and explanations for decline

### Refuting Islamic terrorism

Along with explaining Islamic terrorism, many observers have attempted to point out their inconsistencies and the flaws in their arguments, often suggesting means of de-motivating potential terrorists.

[Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University) Middle Eastern scholar [Bernard Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Lewis) argues that although bin Laden and other radical Islamists claim they are fighting to restore [shariah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shariah) law to the Muslim world, their attacks on civilians violate the classical form of that [Islamic jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_jurisprudence). The "classical jurists of Islam never remotely considered \[jihad] the kind of unprovoked, unannounced mass slaughter of uninvolved civil populations".[^cite_note-254] In regard to the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) Lewis noted,

> Being a religious obligation, jihad is elaborately regulated in sharia law, which discusses in minute detail such matters as the opening, conduct, interruption and cessation of hostilities, the treatment of prisoners and noncombatants, the use of weapons, etc.[^cite_note-255] Similarly, the laws of Jihad categorically preclude wanton and indiscriminate slaughter.[^cite_note-256] The warriors in the holy war are urged not to harm non-combatants, women and children, "unless they attack you first". ... A point on which they insist is the need for a clear declaration of war before beginning hostilities, and for proper warning before resuming hostilities after a truce. What the classical jurists of Islam never remotely considered is the kind of unprovoked, unannounced mass slaughter of uninvolved civil populations that we saw in New York two weeks ago. For this there is no precedent and no authority in Islam.[^cite_note-257]

Similarly, [Timothy Winter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timothy_Winter) writes that the proclamations of bin Laden and [Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_al-Zawahiri) "ignore 14 centuries of Muslim scholarship", and that if they "followed the norms of their religion, they would have had to acknowledge that no school of mainstream Islam allows the targeting of civilians."[^cite_note-258]

Researcher Donald Holbrook notes that while many jihadists quote the beginning of the famous sword verse (or [ayah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayah)):

- But when these months, prohibited (for fighting), are over, slay the idolaters wheresoever you find them, and take them captive or besiege them, and lie in wait for them at every likely place. ...

... they fail to quote and discuss limiting factors that follow,

- ".... But if they repent and fulfill their devotional obligations and pay the zakat, then let them go their way, for God is forgiving and kind."

showing how they are (Holbrook argues) "shamelessly selective in order to serve their propaganda objectives."[^cite_note-holbrook1-20]

The scholarly credentials of the ideologues of extremism are also "questionable".[^cite_note-eikmeier-59] Dale C. Eikmeier notes

> With the exception of [Abul Ala Maududi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abul_Ala_Maududi) and [Abdullah Yusuf Azzam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Yusuf_Azzam), none of Qutbism's main theoreticians trained at Islam's recognized centers of learning. Although a devout Muslim, [Hassan al-Banna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_al-Banna) was a teacher and community activist. [Sayyid Qutb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid_Qutb) was a literary critic. [Mohammed Abdul-Salam Farag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Abdul-Salam_Farag) was an electrician. Ayman al-Zawahiri is a physician. Osama bin Laden trained to be a businessman.[^cite_note-eikmeier-59]

[Michael Sells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Sells) and Jane I. Smith (a professor of Islamic Studies) write that barring some extremists like [al-Qaeda](1921.md), most Muslims do not interpret Qur'anic verses as promoting warfare today but rather as reflecting historical contexts.[^cite_note-Sells-259][^cite_note-260] According to Sells, most Muslims "no more expect to apply" the verses at issue "to their contemporary non-Muslim friends and neighbors than most Christians and Jews consider themselves commanded by God, like the Biblical [Joshua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua), to exterminate the [infidels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infidel)."[^cite_note-Sells-259]

In his book *[No god but God: The Origins, Evolution, and Future of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_god_but_God:_The_Origins,_Evolution,_and_Future_of_Islam)*, [Iranian-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian-American) academic [Reza Aslan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Aslan) argues that there is an internal battle currently taking place within Islam between [individualistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individualism) reform ideals and the [traditional authority of Muslim clerics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama).[^cite_note-david-261] The struggle is similar to that of the 16th-century [reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformation) in [Christianity](5211.md), and in fact is happening when the religion of Islam is as "old" as Christianity was at the time of its reformation.[^cite_note-stan-262] Aslan argues that "the notion that historical context should play no role in the interpretation of the Koran—that what applied to Muhammad's community applies to all Muslim communities for all time—is simply an untenable position in every sense."[^cite_note-times-263]

Despite their proclaimed devotion to the virtue of Sharia law, Jihadists have not always avoided association with the pornography of the despised West. *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* (London) newspaper has pointed out that Jihadists were discovered by one source to have sought anonymity through some of the same dark networks used to distribute [child pornography](25211885.md)—quite ironic given their proclaimed [piety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piety).[^cite_note-Times-264] Similarly, [Reuters](18998750.md) news agency reported that pornography was found among the materials seized from [Osama bin Laden's Abbottabad compound that was raided by U.S. Navy SEALs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Osama_bin_Laden).[^cite_note-265]

Takfir

Despite the fact that a founding principle of modern violent jihad is the defense of Islam and Muslims, most victims of attacks by Islamic terrorism ("the vast majority" according to one source—J.J. Goldberg)[^cite_note-Goldberg-9-11-2017-266] are self-proclaimed Muslims. Many if not all Salafi-Jihadi groups practice [takfir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfir)—i.e. proclaim that some self-proclaimed Muslims (especially government officials and security personnel) are actually apostates deserving of death.

Furthermore, the more learned salafi-jihadi thinkers and leaders are (and were), the more reluctance they are/were to embrace [takfir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfir) (according to a study by Shane Drennan).[^cite_note-DRENNAN-2008-267] The late [Abdullah Yusuf Azzam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Yusuf_Azzam), "the godfather of the Afghan jihad", for example, was an Islamic scholar and university professor who avoided takfir and preached unity in the [ummah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah) (Muslim community). The Islamic education of Al-Qaeda's number two leader, [Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_al-Zawahiri), was early and much more informal—he was not a trained scholar—and al-Zawahiri expanded the definition of kafir to include many self-proclaimed Muslims. He has maintained that civilian government employees of Muslim states, security forces and any persons collaborating or engaging with these groups are apostates, for example.[^cite_note-DRENNAN-2008-267]

Two extreme takfiris -- [Abu Musab al-Zarqawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Musab_al-Zarqawi), a Sunni jihadist leader in Iraq, and [Djamel Zitouni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Djamel_Zitouni), leader of the Algerian [Armed Islamic Group of Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Islamic_Group_of_Algeria) (GIA) during the Algerian civil war—had even broader definitions of apostasy and less religious knowledge. Al-Zarqawi was a petty criminal who had no religious training until he was 22 and limited training thereafter. Famous for bombing targets other jihadis thought off limits,[^cite_note-Cambanis-3-10-15-268] his definition of apostates included all Shia Muslims and "anyone violating his organization's interpretation of Shari'a".[^cite_note-DRENNAN-2008-267] [Djamel Zitouni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Djamel_Zitouni) was the son of a chicken farmer with little Islamic education. He famously expanded the GIA's definition of apostate until he concluded the whole of Algerian society outside of the GIA "had left Islam". His attacks led to the deaths of thousands of Algerian civilians.[^cite_note-DRENNAN-2008-267]

Egypts's largest radical Islamic group, [al-Jama'a al-Islamiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Jama'a_al-Islamiyya) — which killed at least 796 Egyptian policemen and soldiers from 1992 to 1998 — renounced bloodshed in 2003 in a deal with the Egyptian government where a series of high-ranking members were released (as of 2009 "the group has perpetrated no new terrorist acts"). A second group [Egyptian Islamic Jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Islamic_Jihad) made a similar agreement in 2007. Preceding the agreements was program where Muslim scholars debated with imprisoned group leaders arguing that true Islam did not support terrorism.[^cite_note-DeAngelis-2009-269]

## Muslim attitudes toward terrorism

Main article: [Muslim attitudes towards terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_attitudes_towards_terrorism)

The opinions of Muslims on the subject of attacks on civilians by Islamist groups vary. [Fred Halliday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_Halliday), an Irish academic specialist on the Middle East, argues that most Muslims consider these acts to be egregious violations of Islam's laws.[^cite_note-270] Muslims living in the West denounce the 11 September attacks against United States, while Hezbollah contends that their rocket attacks against Israeli targets are [defensive jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defensive_jihad) by a legitimate [resistance movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resistance_movement) rather than terrorism.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272]

### Views of modern Islamic scholars

In reference to suicide attacks, Hannah Stuart notes there is a "significant debate among contemporary clerics over which circumstance permit such attacks." Qatar-based theologian, [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi), criticized the 9/11 attacks but previously justified suicide bombings in Israel on the grounds of necessity and justified such attacks in 2004 against American military and civilian personnel in Iraq. According to Stuart, 61 contemporary Islamic leaders have issued fatawa permitting suicide attacks, 32 with respect to Israel. Stuart points out that all of these contemporary rulings are contrary to classical [Islamic jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_jurisprudence).[^cite_note-Stuart1-273]

[Charles Kurzman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Kurzman) and other authors have collected statements by prominent Muslim figures and organizations condemning terrorism.[^cite_note-kurzman-17] In September 2014, an open letter to ISIS by "over 120 prominent Muslim scholars" denounced that group for "numerous religious transgressions and abominable crimes".[^cite_note-css-274][^cite_note-letter-275]

[Huston Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huston_Smith), an author on comparative religion, argued that extremists have hijacked Islam, just as has occurred periodically in Christianity, [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) and other religions throughout history. He added that the real problem is that extremists do not know their own faith.[^cite_note-276]

[Ali Gomaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Gomaa), former [Grand Mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mufti) of Egypt, stated not only for Islam but in general: "Terrorism cannot be born of religion. Terrorism is the product of corrupt minds, hardened hearts, and arrogant egos, and corruption, destruction, and arrogance are unknown to the heart attached to the divine."[^cite_note-277]

A [600-page legal opinion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatwa_on_Terrorism) (*fatwa*) by [Muhammad Tahir-ul-Qadri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Tahir-ul-Qadri) condemned suicide bombings and other forms of terrorism as *[kufr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kufr)* (unbelief),[^cite_note-278] stating that it "has no place in Islamic teaching and no justification can be provided for it, or any kind of excuses or ifs or buts."[^cite_note-279] Iranian Ayatollah [Ozma Seyyed Yousef Sanei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yousef_Sanei) has preached against suicide attacks and stated in an interview: "Terror in Islam, and especially Shiite, is forbidden."[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281]

A group of Pakistani clerics of [Jamaat Ahl-e-Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaat_Ahle_Sunnat) ([Barelvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barelvi) movement) who were gathered for a convention denounced suicide attacks and beheadings as un-Islamic in a unanimous resolution.[^cite_note-282] On 2 July 2013 in [Lahore](125315.md), 50 Muslim scholars of the Sunni Ittehad Council (SIC) issued a collective fatwa against suicide bombings, the killing of innocent people, bomb attacks, and targeted killings. It considers them to be forbidden.[^cite_note-283]

## Tactics

Main article: [Tactics of terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tactics_of_terrorism)

### Suicide attacks

See also: [Suicide attack](18835454.md)

[Hezbollah](13919.md) was the first group to use suicide bombers in the Middle East.[^cite_note-Islamic_Evolution-47] An increasingly popular tactic used by terrorists is suicide bombing.[^cite_note-284] This tactic is used against civilians, soldiers, and government officials of the regimes the terrorists oppose. A recent clerical ruling declares terrorism and suicide bombing as forbidden by Islam.[^cite_note-285]

### Hijackings

Islamic terrorism sometimes employs the hijacking of passenger vehicles. The most infamous were the ["9/11"](5058690.md) attacks that killed nearly 3,000 people on a single day in 2001, effectively ending the era of [aircraft hijacking](2075.md).

### Hostage taking, kidnappings and executions

Main article: [Beheading in Islamism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beheading_in_Islamism)

See also: [Beheading video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beheading_video) and [ISIL beheading incidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISIL_beheading_incidents)

Along with bombings and hijackings, Islamic terrorists have made extensive use of highly publicised kidnappings and executions (i.e. ritualized murders), often circulating videos of the acts for use as propaganda. A frequent form of execution by these groups is [decapitation](70802.md), another is shooting. In the 1980s, a [series of abductions of American citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanon_hostage_crisis) by Hezbollah during the [Lebanese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Civil_War) resulted in the 1986 [Iran–Contra affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair). During the chaos of the [Iraq War](5043324.md), more than 200 kidnappings [foreign hostages](2520764.md) (for various reasons and by various groups, including purely criminal) gained great international notoriety, even as the great majority (thousands) of victims were Iraqis. In 2007, the [kidnapping of Alan Johnston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidnapping_of_Alan_Johnston) by [Army of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_Islam_\(Gaza_Strip\)) resulted in the British government meeting a Hamas member for the first time.

Motivations

[Islamist](15012.md) militants, including [Boko Haram](23769768.md), [Hamas](13913.md), [al-Qaeda](1921.md) and the [ISIS](9087364.md), have used [kidnapping](169376.md) as a method of fundraising, as a means of bargaining for political concessions, and as a way of intimidating potential opponents.[^cite_note-286]

#### As political tactic

An example of political kidnapping occurred in September 2014, in the Philippines. The German Foreign Ministry reported that the Islamist militant group [Abu Sayyaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sayyaf) had kidnapped two German nationals and was threatening to kill them unless the German government withdraw its support for the war against [ISIS](9087364.md) and also pay a large ransom.[^cite_note-287] In September 2014 an Islamist militant group kidnapped a French national in Algeria and threatened to kill the hostage unless the government of France withdrew its support for the war against ISIS.[^cite_note-288]

##### Islamist self-justifications

ISIL justified various acts internally, including beheadings and its kidnapping of [Yazidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidi) women and forcing them to become sex slaves or concubines.[^cite_note-289]

[Abubakar Shekau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Shekau), the leader of the Nigerian extremist group [Boko Haram](23769768.md), said in a 2014 interview claiming responsibility for the [2014 Chibok kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Chibok_kidnapping) of 270+ schoolgirls, "[Slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_the_Muslim_world) is allowed in my religion, and I shall capture people and make them slaves".[^cite_note-CNNEssenceTerror-290]

#### Kidnapping as revenue

[Nasir al-Wuhayshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasir_al-Wuhayshi) leader of the [Islamist](15012.md) militant group [Al-Qaida in the Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaida_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula) describes kidnapped hostages as "an easy spoil... which I may describe as a profitable trade and a precious treasure."[^cite_note-nytimes1-291]

A 2014 investigation, by journalist [Rukmini Maria Callimachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rukmini_Maria_Callimachi) published in *[The New York Times](30680.md)* demonstrated that between 2008 and 2014, Al Qaeda and groups directly affiliated with al-Qaeda took in over US\$125 million from kidnapping, with \$66 million of that total paid in 2013 alone. The article showed that from a somewhat haphazard beginning in 2003, kidnapping grew into the group's main fundraising strategy, with targeted, professional kidnapping of civilians from wealthy European countries—principally France, Spain and Switzerland—willing to pay huge ransoms. US and UK nationals are less commonly targeted since these governments have shown an unwillingness to pay ransom.[^cite_note-nytimes1-291]

[Boko Haram](23769768.md) kidnapped Europeans for the Ransom their governments would pay in the early 2010s.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294]

According to [Yochi Dreazen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yochi_Dreazen) writing in *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)*, although ISIS received funding from Qatar, Kuwait and other Gulf oil states, "traditional criminal techniques like kidnapping", are a key funding source for ISIS.[^cite_note-295] [Armin Rosen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armin_Rosen?action=edit\&redlink=1) writing in [Business Insider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Insider), kidnapping was a "crucial early source" of funds as ISIS expanded rapidly in 2013.[^cite_note-296] In March, upon receiving payment from the government of Spain, ISIS released 2 Spanish hostages working for the newspaper [El Mundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Mundo_\(Spain\)), correspondent Javier Espinosa and photographer [Ricardo Garcia Vilanova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Garcia_Vilanova), who had been held since September 2013.[^cite_note-297] [Philip Balboni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Balboni?action=edit\&redlink=1), CEO of [GlobalPost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GlobalPost) told the press that he had spent "millions" in efforts to ransom journalist [James Foley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Foley_\(journalist\)), and an American official told the [Associated Press](18935732.md) that demand from ISIS was for 100 million (\$132.5).[^cite_note-298] In September 2014, following the release of ISIS [Beheading videos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beheading_video) of journalists James Foley and [Steven Sotloff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Sotloff), British Prime Minister [David Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cameron) appealed to members of the [G7](1196634.md) to abide by their pledges not to pay ransom "in the case of terrorist kidnap".[^cite_note-299]

Holding foreign journalists as hostages is so valuable to ISIS that Rami Jarrah, a Syrian who has acted as go-between in efforts to ransom foreign hostages, told the *[Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street_Journal)* that ISIS had "made it known" to other militant groups that they "would pay" for kidnapped journalists.[^cite_note-autogenerated5-300] ISIS has also kidnapped foreign-aid workers and Syrians who work for foreign-funded groups and reconstruction projects in Syria.[^cite_note-autogenerated5-300] By mid-2014, ISIS was holding assets valued at US\$2 billion.[^cite_note-301]

##### Kidnapping as psychological warfare

Boko Haram has been described as using kidnapping as a means of intimidating the civilian population into non-resistance.[^cite_note-ctc.usma.edu-302][^cite_note-Marina_Lazreg_2009_p._47-303]

According to psychologist [Irwin Mansdorf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irwin_Mansdorf?action=edit\&redlink=1), Hamas demonstrated effectiveness of kidnapping as a form of [psychological warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_warfare) in the [2006 capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Gaza_cross-border_raid) of the Israeli soldier [Gilad Shalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilad_Shalit) when public pressure forced the government of Israel to release 1027 prisoners, including 280 convicted of terrorism by Israel, in exchange for his release.[^cite_note-304] According to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, "Hamas has recognized the pull such incidents have over the Israeli psyche and clearly has moved to grab hostages in incidents such as the [death and ransoming of Oron Shaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_ransoming_of_Oron_Shaul)."[^cite_note-305]

### Internet recruiting

Main article: [Terrorism and social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_and_social_media)

In the beginning of the 21st century, emerged a worldwide network of hundreds of web sites that inspire, train, educate and recruit young Muslims to engage in jihad against the United States and other Western countries, taking less prominent roles in mosques and community centers that are under scrutiny.

## Examples of organizations

Main article: [List of Islamist terrorist attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Islamist_terrorist_attacks)

Further information: [List of designated terrorist groups](63578.md)

See also: [United States Department of State list of Foreign Terrorist Organizations](421835.md), [Boko Haram insurgency](27868253.md), and [1998 United States embassy bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_United_States_embassy_bombings)



The "black flag of [Jihad](16203.md)", used by various Islamist organizations since the late 1990s, consists of a white-on-black [shahada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada).

### Africa

The African continent has been affected by significant Islamist terrorist activity across the continent, involving various militant groups responsible for widespread violence and instability.[^cite_note-306]\[^cite_note-Welle\_[www.dw.com-307](http://www.dw.com-307/)][^cite_note-thedefensepost.com-308] In [East Africa](260987.md), [Al-Shabaab](8726463.md)[^cite_note-309] has been a central figure in conflicts, particularly in [Somalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Somalia) and [Kenya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Kenya).[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] [North](21714.md) and [West Africa](67393.md) have seen major incidents tied to groups like [Boko Haram](23769768.md),[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-thedefensepost.com-308][^cite_note-reuters.com-313] which has caused severe disruptions in [Nigeria](21383.md) and neighboring countries. Additionally, nations such as [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Egypt),[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316] [Algeria](358.md), [Tunisia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_terrorist_incidents_in_Tunisia),[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] and [Morocco](19291.md)[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321] have experienced deadly attacks linked to broader extremist networks. These activities have led to [extensive casualties and displacement across the region](56119804.md). Civilians have been the main targets of terrorist attacks by Islamist militants.[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323][^cite_note-confirm-324]

### Asia

In [Afghanistan](15361156.md), [Taliban](30635.md) and [Hezb-e-Islami Gulbuddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezb-e_Islami_Gulbuddin) forces have attacked civilians.[^cite_note-325] Kyrgyz-American brothers were behind the [Boston Marathon bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Marathon_bombing). In [Tajikistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Tajikistan), the IMU was blamed for a 2010 suicide bombing that killed two policemen.[^cite_note-326] [Uzbekistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Uzbekistan) saw multiple attacks,[^cite_note-Rfe2004-07-27-327][^cite_note-rferl.org-328] including the [1999 Tashkent bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Tashkent_bombings) targeting President [Karimov](335230.md).[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-CORNELL-330] [China has faced attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_China) linked to [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) [separatism](286527.md). In [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Bangladesh), [Jamaat-ul-Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaat-ul-Mujahideen_Bangladesh)[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] and [Ansarullah Bangla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansarullah_Bangla_Team)[^cite_note-333] have been involved in bombings and attacks on activists. In [India](14533.md), [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md)[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] and [Jaish-e-Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed)[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337] are responsible for numerous attacks,[^cite_note-338] while [Pakistan](2816470.md) and [Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Sri_Lanka) have also faced Islamist attacks.[^cite_note-339] The [Abu Sayyaf Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sayyaf)[^cite_note-24threlease-340] in the [Philippines](9101671.md) continued a long-running [insurgency](589841.md) until 2024.[^cite_note-TKBAbu-341][^cite_note-24threlease-340]

### Europe

Main articles: [Islamic terrorism in Europe](51245422.md) and [Al-Qaeda activities in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_activities_in_Europe)

See also: [Foreign fighters in the Syrian and Iraqi Civil Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_fighters_in_the_Syrian_and_Iraqi_Civil_Wars)

Planned and foiled Jihadist terror attacks in Europe. Journal of Peace Research, revised figures.[^cite_note-342]\
&#x20;View [chart definition](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Data\:Planned%20and%20foiled%20Jihadist%20terror%20attacks%20in%20Europe.chart).

The Europe has seen significant Islamist terrorist attacks,[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-Europol17-344][^cite_note-Europol18-345][^cite_note-346] including the [2004 Madrid bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Madrid_train_bombings),[^cite_note-Great_Disaster-347][^cite_note-Elizabeth_Nash-348] the [2005 London bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_July_2005_London_bombings),[^cite_note-lucky-driver-349][^cite_note-north-diary-350] and the [2015 Paris attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2015_Paris_attacks),[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-autogenerated1-352][^cite_note-autogenerated2-353] all of which caused numerous casualties. A report highlights that jihadist terrorism has been responsible for the majority of deaths from terrorism in Europe from 2001 to 2014.[^cite_note-354] It also discusses how jihadists in Europe have financed their activities through petty crime and how terrorist attacks have increasingly targeted people rather than property.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] Countries like [Belgium](54343607.md),[^cite_note-arab-357][^cite_note-Bbc20051202-358] [Netherlands](21148.md),[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-life_imprisonment-360][^cite_note-361] [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_France),[^cite_note-ispi2018-362][^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] and [Germany](35484188.md) have faced multiple attacks,[^cite_note-365] with Belgium being a significant base for planning attacks like the [2015 Paris attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2015_Paris_attacks). Despite proximity to conflict zones, [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Italy) has had relatively fewer jihadist incidents.[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] The EU report found that most Islamist terror suspects were second or third-generation [immigrants](4599312.md),[^cite_note-Europol-2009a-368] while over 99% of attempted terrorist attacks in Europe in the past three years were committed by non-Muslims.[^cite_note-Europol-2009a-368]<sup>: 48 </sup>

In [Norway](41948345.md), two men were sentenced in 2012 under anti-terror legislation for plotting against cartoonist Kurt Westergaard.[^cite_note-369] [Russia](25391.md) has faced politically and religiously motivated attacks,[^cite_note-370] particularly in Chechnya, including the [2002 Moscow theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_theater_hostage_crisis)[^cite_note-Satter-371][^cite_note-372] and [2004 Beslan school hostage crises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beslan_school_siege),[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] linked to groups like the [Caucasus Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Emirate). [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Spain) has seen jihadist activity since the 1990s, highlighted by the [2004 Madrid train bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Madrid_train_bombings)[^cite_note-Great_Disaster-347][^cite_note-Elizabeth_Nash-348] and the [2017 Barcelona attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Barcelona_attacks).[^cite_note-BBCWhatVictims-375] In [Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Sweden), terrorist incidents have occurred like the [2010 Stockholm bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Stockholm_bombings)[^cite_note-cnn-376][^cite_note-bbc-377][^cite_note-378] and the [2017 Stockholm truck attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Stockholm_truck_attack).[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380]

### Middle East

Militant Islamism in [Turkey](11125639.md) primarily has [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_nationalism) roots,[^cite_note-ReferenceA-381] with groups like [Turkish Hizbullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_Hezbollah)[^cite_note-382] and [İBDA-C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Eastern_Islamic_Raiders'_Front)[^cite_note-383] opposing Turkish secularism. [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Iraq) has experienced terrorist activity,[^cite_note-384] especially during the [US-led Iraq War](5043324.md).[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386] In [Palestine](241405.md) and [Israeli](9282173.md) territories, groups such as [Hamas](13913.md)[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] and the [Islamic Jihad Movement](13093253.md)[^cite_note-390] have launched numerous attacks against Israel. Additionally, [Fatah al-Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah_al-Islam)[^cite_note-Figaro_20070416-391] operates in [Lebanon](17771.md) with goals of establishing Islamic law in [Palestinian refugee camps](400347.md).[^cite_note-Reuters_FactBox-392][^cite_note-IHT_20070315-393]

### Americas

In [Canada](936572.md), the government identifies terrorism as a significant threat,[^cite_note-394] particularly emphasizing domestic radicalization,[^cite_note-395] as seen in the [2006 Ontario terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Ontario_terrorism_plot) plot involving an [Al-Qaeda](1921.md)-inspired cell.[^cite_note-396] In the [United States](18563588.md), the [9/11 attacks](5058690.md) resulted in nearly 3,000 fatalities[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-397] and initiated the [war on terror](13425800.md), with homegrown extremism highlighted as a major concern.[^cite_note-Hayden-398][^cite_note-399] [Australia](9971246.md) and [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_New_Zealand) have also experienced notable attacks, such as the [Lindt Café siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindt_Cafe_siege)[^cite_note-Ensor-400][^cite_note-SMHInquest-401] and the [Auckland supermarket stabbing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Auckland_supermarket_stabbing).[^cite_note-1News3Sept-402][^cite_note-AP1-403][^cite_note-404] In [Argentina](45331227.md), the [1992 bombing of the Israeli embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Buenos_Aires_Israeli_embassy_bombing)[^cite_note-405] and the [1994 AMIA bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMIA_bombing)[^cite_note-BBC_Iran-406][^cite_note-407] were linked to terrorist groups and Iran,[^cite_note-La_Nación_October_2006-408] resulting in significant casualties and political repercussions.

### Transnational

Main article: [Al-Qaeda](1921.md)

[Al-Qaeda](1921.md)'s stated aim is the use of jihad to defend and protect Islam against [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism), [Christianity](5211.md), the secular West, and Muslim governments such as Saudi Arabia, which it sees as insufficiently Islamic and too closely tied to the United States.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410][^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412]

Significantly, Abd al-Wahhab also insisted that it was a sign of spiritual weakness for Muslims to care for or be interested in non-Muslim beliefs or practices. Pursuant to a doctrine known as *al-wala' wa al-bara'* (literally, the doctrine of loyalty and disassociation), Abd al-Wahhab argued that it was imperative for Muslims not to befriend, ally themselves with, or imitate non-Muslims or heretical Muslims. Furthermore, this enmity and hostility of Muslims toward non-Muslims and heretical had to be visible and unequivocal. For example, by not being the first to greet a non-Muslim, orby ever wishing a non-Muslim peace.
